    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.42} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q55}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $G$ be a simple, finite, undirected graph with vertex set $\{v_1, \dots, v_n\}$. Let $\Delta(G)$ denote the maximum degree of $G$ and let $\mathbb{N} = \{1, 2, \dots\}$ denote the set of all possible colors. Color the vertices of $G$ using the following greedy strategy:
\begin{lstlisting}
for i = 1, ..., n
    color(vi) <- min{j in N : no neighbour of vi is colored j}
\end{lstlisting}
Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} This procedure results in a proper vertex coloring of $G$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The number of colors used is at most $\Delta(G) + 1$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The number of colors used is at most $\Delta(G)$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The number of colors used is equal to the chromatic number of $G$.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A, B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Graphs: connectivity, matching, colouring}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The greedy coloring strategy assigns to each vertex the lowest index color not used by any already-colored neighbor, guaranteeing that adjacent vertices receive distinct colors (proper coloring, so A is TRUE). Because any vertex has degree at most $\Delta(G)$, at most $\Delta(G)$ colors are forbidden at any step, ensuring that at most $\Delta(G) + 1$ colors are ever needed (B is TRUE). Greedy coloring does not guarantee finding the minimum chromatic number.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.43} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q32}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $A$ and $B$ be non-empty finite sets such that there exist one-to-one and onto functions (i) from $A$ to $B$ and (ii) from $A \times A$ to $A \cup B$. The number of possible values of $|A|$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 2 to 2} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Sets, relations, functions, partial orders and lattices}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A bijection from $A$ to $B$ implies $|A| = |B| = n$. A bijection from $A \times A$ to $A \cup B$ implies $|A \times A| = |A \cup B|$. We have $|A \times A| = n^2$, and $n \le |A \cup B| \le 2n$. Thus $n \le n^2 \le 2n$. Since $A$ is non-empty ($n \ge 1$), dividing by $n$ gives $1 \le n \le 2$. This allows exactly two integer solutions: $n = 1$ (where $A = B$) and $n = 2$ (where $A \cap B = \emptyset$).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.44} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q51}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The chromatic number of a graph is the minimum number of colours used in a proper colouring of the graph. Let $G$ be any graph with $n$ vertices and chromatic number $k$. Which of the following statements is/are always TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $G$ contains a complete subgraph with $k$ vertices \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $G$ contains an independent set of size at least $n/k$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $G$ contains at least $k(k-1)/2$ edges \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $G$ contains a vertex of degree at least $k$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Graphs: connectivity, matching, colouring}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A proper $k$-coloring partitions the $n$ vertices into $k$ independent sets. By the Pigeonhole Principle, at least one of these independent sets has size $\ge \lceil n/k \rceil \ge n/k$ (B is TRUE). In addition, every pair of color classes must have at least one edge between them (otherwise those two colors could be merged, yielding a $(k-1)$-coloring), requiring at least $\binom{k}{2} = \frac{k(k-1)}{2}$ edges (C is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.45} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q52}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the operators $\diamond$ and $\square$ defined by $a \diamond b = a + 2b$, $a \square b = ab$, for positive integers. Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Operator $\diamond$ obeys the associative law \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Operator $\square$ obeys the associative law \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Operator $\diamond$ over the operator $\square$ obeys the distributive law \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Operator $\square$ over the operator $\diamond$ obeys the distributive law
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Monoids, Groups}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For $\square$: $a \square b = ab$, which is associative since $(ab)c = a(bc)$ (B is TRUE). Distributivity of $\square$ over $\diamond$: $a \square (b \diamond c) = a(b + 2c) = ab + 2ac$. Comparing with $(a \square b) \diamond (a \square c) = (ab) \diamond (ac) = ab + 2(ac) = ab + 2ac$; both sides match identically, so $\square$ distributes over $\diamond$ (D is TRUE). For $\diamond$: $(a \diamond b) \diamond c = a + 2b + 2c \neq a + 2b + 4c = a \diamond (b \diamond c)$, so $\diamond$ is not associative.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.46} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q17}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $A$ be the adjacency matrix of a simple undirected graph $G$. Suppose $A$ is its own inverse. Which one of the following statements is always TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $G$ is a cycle & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $G$ is a perfect matching \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $G$ is a complete graph & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} There is no such graph $G$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Graphs: connectivity, matching, colouring}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small If $A = A^{-1}$, then $A^2 = I$. The $(i, j)$-th entry of $A^2$ is the number of walks of length 2 between vertex $i$ and vertex $j$. Thus $(A^2)_{ii} = \text{deg}(i) = 1$ for every vertex $i$, and $(A^2)_{ij} = 0$ for $i \neq j$. Every vertex has degree exactly 1, which defines a 1-regular graph, i.e., a perfect matching.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.47} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q34}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $P$ be the partial order defined on the set $\{1,2,3,4\}$ as follows:
\[ P = \{(x, x) \mid x \in \{1,2,3,4\}\} \cup \{(1,2), (3,2), (3,4)\} \]
The number of total orders on $\{1,2,3,4\}$ that contain $P$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 5 to 5} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Sets, relations, functions, partial orders and lattices}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The poset constraints are: $1 < 2$, $3 < 2$, and $3 < 4$. Any compatible total order is a linear extension of this DAG. Elements 1 and 3 must precede 2, while 3 must precede 4. Counting topological orderings of $\{1, 2, 3, 4\}$ satisfying these constraints yields exactly 5 total orders: (1,3,2,4), (1,3,4,2), (3,1,2,4), (3,1,4,2), (3,4,1,2).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.48} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q17}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

$g(\cdot)$ is a function from $A$ to $B$, $f(\cdot)$ is a function from $B$ to $C$, and their composition defined as $f(g(\cdot))$ is a mapping from $A$ to $C$.
If $f(\cdot)$ and $f(g(\cdot))$ are onto (surjective) functions, which ONE of the following is TRUE about the function $g(\cdot)$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $g(\cdot)$ must be an onto (surjective) function. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $g(\cdot)$ must be a one-to-one (injective) function. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $g(\cdot)$ must be a bijective function, that is, both one-to-one and onto. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $g(\cdot)$ is not required to be a one-to-one or onto function.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Sets, relations, functions, partial orders and lattices}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small If the composition $f \circ g: A \to C$ is surjective, $f$ must be surjective, but $g$ is not required to be either injective or surjective. For example, if $C$ is a singleton $\{c\}$ and $B = \{b_1, b_2\}$, $g$ can map all of $A$ to $b_1$ where $f(b_1) = c$, leaving $b_2$ uncovered (not onto) and having many collisions (not one-to-one).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.49} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q30}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $S$ be the set of all ternary strings defined over the alphabet $\{a, b, c\}$. Consider all strings in $S$ that contain at least one occurrence of two consecutive symbols, that is, ``aa'', ``bb'' or ``cc''. The number of such strings of length 5 that are possible is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 195 to 195} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Combinatorics: counting, recurrence relations, generating functions}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The total number of ternary strings of length 5 is $3^5 = 243$. A string has no consecutive identical symbols if the first character has 3 choices and each subsequent character has 2 choices (different from the preceding character), giving $3 \times 2^4 = 48$ such strings. By complementary counting, the number of strings with at least one consecutive pair is $243 - 48 = 195$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.50} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q48}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following predicate logic formulae/formula is/are CORRECT representation(s) of the statement: ``Everyone has exactly one mother''?
The meanings of the predicates used are:
\begin{itemize}
\item $mother(y, x)$: $y$ is the mother of $x$
\item $noteq(x, y)$: $x$ and $y$ are not equal
\end{itemize}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\forall x \exists y \exists z (mother(y, x) \land \neg mother(z, x))$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\forall x \exists y [mother(y, x) \land \forall z (noteq(z, y) \to \neg mother(z, x))]$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\forall x \forall y [mother(y, x) \to \exists z (mother(z, x) \land \neg noteq(z, y))]$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\forall x \exists y [mother(y, x) \land \neg \exists z (noteq(z, y) \land mother(z, x))]$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Propositional and first order logic}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small ``Exactly one mother'' requires existence ($\exists y \, mother(y, x)$) and uniqueness (any $z \neq y$ cannot be a mother of $x$). Option B asserts $\forall z (z \neq y \to \neg mother(z, x))$, and Option D asserts $\neg \exists z (z \neq y \land mother(z, x))$, which are logically equivalent formulations of uniqueness.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.51} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q49}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

$A = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots \}$ is the set of non-negative integers. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of functions from $A$ to itself. For any two functions $f_1, f_2 \in \mathcal{F}$, we define
\[ (f\_1 \odot f\_2)(n) = f\_1(n) + f\_2(n) \]
for every number $n$ in $A$. Which of the following is/are CORRECT about the mathematical structure $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is an Abelian group. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is an Abelian monoid. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is a non-Abelian group. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ is a non-Abelian monoid.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Discrete Mathematics \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Monoids, Groups}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Addition on non-negative integers is closed, associative, and commutative, so $\odot$ is associative and commutative. The identity function is $e(n) = 0$ for all $n \in A$, making $(\mathcal{F}, \odot)$ a commutative (Abelian) monoid. Inverses do not exist because negative function values are not in $A$, so it is not a group.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\vspace{8pt}

\textbf{Q.52} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q33}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the unsigned 8-bit fixed point binary number representation below:
\[ b\_7 b\_6 b\_5 b\_4 b\_3 \, . \, b\_2 b\_1 b\_0 \]
where the position of the binary point is between $b_3$ and $b_2$. Which of the following decimal numbers can be represented exactly in this format?
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item 0.500
    \item 0.875
    \item 0.100
    \item 0.001
\end{enumerate}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (i) and (ii) only & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (i) and (iii) only \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (i) and (ii) only & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (i), (ii) and (iii)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Number representations and computer arithmetic (fixed and floating point)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The fractional portion uses 3 bits ($b_2 b_1 b_0$), so the fractional precision is $2^{-3} = 0.125$. Any representable fraction must be an integer multiple of $0.125$: $0.500 = 4 \times 0.125$ (i) and $0.875 = 7 \times 0.125$ (ii) are exact multiples, while $0.100$ and $0.001$ are not.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.53} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q18}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $R_1$ and $R_2$ be two 4-bit registers that store numbers in 2's complement form. For the operation $R_1 + R_2$, which one of the following values of $R_1$ and $R_2$ gives an arithmetic overflow?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $R_1 = 1011$ and $R_2 = 1110$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $R_1 = 1100$ and $R_2 = 1010$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $R_1 = 0011$ and $R_2 = 0100$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $R_1 = 1001$ and $R_2 = 1111$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Number representations and computer arithmetic (fixed and floating point)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In a 4-bit 2's complement system, representable integers span $[-2^3, 2^3 - 1] = [-8, +7]$. For $R_1 = 1100_2 = -4$ and $R_2 = 1010_2 = -6$, their sum is $-4 + (-6) = -10$. Because $-10 < -8$, an arithmetic underflow (overflow in signed representation) occurs.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.54} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q41}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider three floating point numbers A, B and C stored in registers RA, RB and RC, respectively as per IEEE-754 single precision floating point format. The 32-bit content stored in these registers (in hexadecimal form) are as follows:
\[ RA = \text{0xC1400000}, \quad RB = \text{0x42100000}, \quad RC = \text{0x41400000} \]
Which one of the following is FALSE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $A + C = 0$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $C = A + B$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $B = 3C$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $(B - C) > 0$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Number representations and computer arithmetic (fixed and floating point)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Decoding 32-bit IEEE-754: $RA = \text{0xC1400000} = -1.5 \times 2^{130-127} = -12.0$; $RB = \text{0x42100000} = +1.125 \times 2^{132-127} = +36.0$; $RC = \text{0x41400000} = +1.5 \times 2^{130-127} = +12.0$. Evaluating option B: $A + B = -12 + 36 = 24 \neq C = 12.0$, so statement B is FALSE.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.55} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q45}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the IEEE-754 single precision floating point numbers $P = \text{0xC1800000}$ and $Q = \text{0x3F5C2EF4}$.
Which one of the following corresponds to the product of these numbers (i.e., $P \times Q$), represented in the IEEE-754 single precision format?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 0x404C2EF4 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 0x405C2EF4 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 0xC15C2EF4 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 0xC14C2EF4
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Number representations and computer arithmetic (fixed and floating point)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Number $P = \text{0xC1800000}$ has sign bit 1, biased exponent $131$, actual exponent $131 - 127 = 4$, and mantissa $1.0$, so $P = -16.0 = -2^4$. Number $Q = \text{0x3F5C2EF4}$ is positive with biased exponent $126$ (exponent $-1$) and mantissa fraction `5C2EF4`. Multiplying $P \times Q$ flips the sign to negative (1), adds 4 to the exponent (biased exponent becomes $126 + 4 = 130 = \text{0x82}$), and retains the mantissa fraction, resulting in `0xC15C2EF4`.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.56} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q13}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a system that uses 5 bits for representing signed integers in 2's complement format. In this system, two integers $A$ and $B$ are represented as $A = 01010$ and $B = 11010$. Which one of the following operations will result in either an arithmetic overflow or an arithmetic underflow?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $A + B$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $A - B$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $B - A$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $2 * B$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Number representations and computer arithmetic (fixed and floating point)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In a 5-bit 2's complement representation, the range is $[-2^4, 2^4 - 1] = [-16, +15]$. Here, $A = 01010_2 = +10$ and $B = 11010_2 = -6$. The operation $A - B = 10 - (-6) = +16$, which exceeds $+15$, resulting in an arithmetic overflow.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.57} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q28}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the circuit shown below where the gates may have propagation delays. Assume that all signal transitions occur instantaneously and that wires have no delays. Which of the following statements about the circuit is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} With no propagation delays, the output $Y$ is always logic Zero \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} With no propagation delays, the output $Y$ is always logic One \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} With propagation delays, the output $Y$ can have a transient logic One after $X$ transitions from logic Zero to logic One \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} With propagation delays, the output $Y$ can have a transient logic Zero after $X$ transitions from logic One to logic Zero
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Combinational and sequential circuits}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The circuit computes $Y = X \cdot \overline{X}$. In steady-state with no gate delays, $X \cdot \overline{X} = 0$, so $Y$ is always logic Zero (A is TRUE). When $X$ transitions from 0 to 1, if the inverter has a non-zero propagation delay, both inputs to the AND gate remain logic 1 for the duration of the inverter delay, producing a transient logic 1 glitch (C is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.58} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $X$ be a 3-variable Boolean function that produces output as `1' when at least two of the input variables are `1'. Which of the following statement(s) is/are CORRECT, where $a, b, c, d, e$ are Boolean variables?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $X(a, b, X(c, d, e)) = X(X(a, b, c), d, e)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $X(a, b, X(a, b, c)) = X(a, b, c)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $X(a, b, X(a, c, d)) = (X(a, b, a) \text{ AND } X(c, d, c))$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $X(a, b, c) = X(a, X(a, b, c), X(a, c, c))$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Boolean algebra}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The majority function is $X(a, b, c) = ab + bc + ca$. In (B), $X(a, b, M) = ab + bM + aM = ab + (a+b)M = ab + (a+b)(ab+bc+ca) = ab + bc + ca = X(a, b, c)$, so (B) holds. In (D), $X(a, c, c) = c$, giving $X(a, M, c) = aM + Mc + ca = (a+c)M + ac = (a+c)(ab+bc+ca) + ac = ab + bc + ca = X(a, b, c)$, so (D) holds.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

    underlay={

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.59} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q42}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following four variable Boolean function in sum-of-product form:
\[ F(b\_3, b\_2, b\_1, b\_0) = \sum(0, 2, 4, 8, 10, 11, 12) \]
where the value of the function is computed by considering $b_3b_2b_1b_0$ as a 4-bit binary number, where $b_3$ denotes the most significant bit and $b_0$ denotes the least significant bit. Note that there are no don't care terms. Which ONE of the following options is the CORRECT minimized expression?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0 + \overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0 + b_1\overline{b}_2b_3$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0 + \overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0 + b_1b_2b_3$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\overline{b}_0\overline{b}_2 + \overline{b}_3$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Minimization}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Grouping minterms on a 4-variable K-map: minterms $\{0, 2, 8, 10\}$ form a 4-cell group giving $\overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0$; minterms $\{0, 4, 8, 12\}$ form a 4-cell group giving $\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0$; remaining minterm 11 combines with 10 to give $b_3\overline{b}_2b_1$. Combining these essential prime implicants yields $\overline{b}_1\overline{b}_0 + \overline{b}_2\overline{b}_0 + b_1\overline{b}_2b_3$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.60} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q31}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the logic circuit diagram for function $F$. Which is/are the CORRECT option(s) for the output function $F$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\overline{X \overline{Y}}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\overline{X} + \overline{Y} + X\overline{Y}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\overline{X \overline{Y}} + \overline{X} + X\overline{Y}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $X + \overline{Y}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Combinational and sequential circuits: logic gates}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small From Boolean reduction, $\overline{X \cdot \overline{Y}} = \overline{X} + Y$. Applying consensus and absorption theorems yields equivalent representations including $\overline{X} + \overline{Y} + X\overline{Y}$ and $\overline{X \cdot \overline{Y}} + \overline{X} + X\overline{Y}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.61} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q34}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

In a 4-bit ripple counter, if the period of the waveform at the last flip-flop is $64\,\mu\text{s}$, then the frequency of the ripple counter in kHz is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 250 to 250} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Combinational and sequential circuits: ripple counters}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A 4-bit ripple counter divides the clock frequency by $2^4 = 16$. The clock period is $T_{clk} = \frac{T_{out}}{16} = \frac{64\,\mu\text{s}}{16} = 4\,\mu\text{s}$, giving clock frequency $f = \frac{1}{4\,\mu\text{s}} = 250\,\text{kHz}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.62} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q43}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Given the Karnaugh Map for a Boolean function $F(w, x, y, z)$, which one or more of the following Boolean expressions represent $F$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + \overline{w}\overline{x}y\overline{z} + w\overline{x}y\overline{z} + xz$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + \overline{w}\overline{x}y\overline{z} + w\overline{x}yz + xz$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\overline{w}\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}\overline{z} + w\overline{x}\overline{y}z + xz$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\overline{x}\overline{z} + xz$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Boolean algebra and minimization: Karnaugh Map}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Grouping the 1s in the K-map: the four corners and the central groupings give prime implicants $\overline{x}\overline{z} + xz$. Both the expanded minterm expression and minimal SOP are valid representations.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --

    }
]
\textbf{Q.63} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q50}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following Boolean algebraic equation(s) is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\overline{A}BC + A\overline{B}\overline{C} + \overline{A}\overline{B}\overline{C} + A\overline{B}C + ABC = BC + \overline{B}\overline{C} + \overline{A}\overline{B}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $AB + \overline{A}C + BC = AB + \overline{A}C$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $(A + C)(\overline{A} + B) = AB + \overline{A}C$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\overline{(A + \overline{B} + \overline{D})(C + D)(\overline{A} + C + D)(A + B + \overline{D})} = \overline{A}D + \overline{C}\overline{D}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Digital Logic \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Boolean algebra and minimization: identities and consensus theorem}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Equation B is the classic Consensus Theorem: $AB + \overline{A}C + BC = AB + \overline{A}C$. Equation C expands to $AB + \overline{A}C$, and Equation D simplifies via De Morgan's laws and complementation to $\overline{A}D + \overline{C}\overline{D}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.64} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A 32-bit wide main memory unit with a capacity of 1 GB is built using $256\text{M} \times 4\text{-bit}$ DRAM chips. The number of rows of memory cells in the DRAM chip is $2^{14}$. The time taken to perform one refresh operation is 50 nanoseconds. The refresh period is 2 milliseconds. The percentage of time that the memory is available for performing the normal read and write operations is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\%. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 59.0 to 60.0} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The DRAM has $2^{14} = 16384$ rows. Refreshing all rows takes $16384 \times 50\text{ ns} = 819.2\ \mu\text{s}$ every $2000\ \mu\text{s}$. The refresh overhead fraction is $\frac{819.2}{2000} = 40.96\%$. Hence, the percentage of time available for reading and writing memory is $100\% - 40.96\% = 59.04\% \approx 59\%$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.65} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q34}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The size of the physical address space of a processor is $2^P$ bytes. The word length is $2^W$ bytes. The capacity of cache memory is $2^N$ bytes. The size of each cache block is $2^M$ words. For a $K$-way set-associative cache memory, the length (in number of bits) of the tag field is

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $P - N - \log_2 K$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $P - N + \log_2 K$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $P - N - M - W - \log_2 K$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $P - N - M - W + \log_2 K$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Block size $= 2^{M+W}$ bytes $\implies M+W$ offset bits. Cache size $= 2^N$ bytes, so number of lines $= 2^N / 2^{M+W} = 2^{N-M-W}$. With $K = 2^k$ lines per set, number of sets $= 2^{N-M-W-k} \implies N-M-W-k$ set index bits. Tag bits $= P - (N-M-W-k) - (M+W) = P - N + k = P - N + \log_2 K$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside align=top,

    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.66} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q50}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The instruction pipeline of a RISC processor has five stages: Instruction Fetch (IF), Instruction Decode (ID), Perform Operation (PO), Operand Read (OR), and Write Back (WB). Each stage takes 1 clock cycle except PO. For a sequence of 100 instructions, 40 instructions require 3 clock cycles in PO, 35 instructions require 2 clock cycles in PO, and the remaining 25 instructions require 1 clock cycle in PO. Assuming no memory stalls or branch delays, the total number of clock cycles to execute the 100 instructions is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_.

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 219 to 219} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Instruction pipelining, pipeline hazards}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For 100 instructions in a 5-stage pipeline without branch delays, ideal cycles $= 5 + (100 - 1) = 104$. The PO stage causes pipeline stalls when operand evaluation takes extra cycles: 40 instructions require 3 cycles (2 extra cycles $\implies 40 \times 2 = 80$ stall cycles) and 35 instructions require 2 cycles (1 extra cycle $\implies 35 \times 1 = 35$ stall cycles). Total clock cycles $= 104 + 80 + 35 = 219$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.67} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let WB and WT be two set associative cache organizations that use LRU algorithm for cache block replacement. WB is a write back cache and WT is a write through cache. Which of the following statements is/are FALSE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Each cache block in WB and WT has a dirty bit. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Every write hit in WB leads to a data transfer from cache to main memory. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Eviction of a block from WT will not lead to data transfer from cache to main memory. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} A read miss in WB will never lead to eviction of a dirty block from WB.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A,B,D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Write-through (WT) caches never maintain dirty blocks because main memory is updated simultaneously on every write, so WT does not have dirty bits (A is FALSE). A write hit in write-back (WB) only updates the cache and sets the dirty bit, not memory (B is FALSE). On a read miss in WB, if the victim block chosen for replacement is dirty, it must be written back to memory (D is FALSE). Statements A, B, and D are FALSE.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.68} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q33}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A cache memory that has a hit rate of 0.8 has an access latency 10 ns and miss penalty 100 ns. An optimization is done on the cache to reduce the miss rate. However, the optimization results in an increase of cache access latency to 15 ns, whereas the miss penalty is not affected. The minimum hit rate (rounded off to two decimal places) needed after the optimization such that it should not increase the average memory access time is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in numeric format)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 0.85 to 0.85} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Initial AMAT is $10 + (1 - 0.80) \times 100 = 10 + 20 = 30\text{ ns}$. With the new access latency of $15\text{ ns}$, we require $15 + (1 - h) \times 100 \le 30 \implies 100(1 - h) \le 15 \implies 1 - h \le 0.15 \implies h \ge 0.85$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.69} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q61}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A processor X1 operating at 2 GHz has a standard 5-stage RISC instruction pipeline having a base CPI (cycles per instruction) of one without any pipeline hazards. For a given program P that has 30\% branch instructions, control hazards incur 2 cycles stall for every branch. A new version of the processor X2 operating at same clock frequency has an additional branch predictor unit (BPU) that completely eliminates stalls for correctly predicted branches. There is neither any savings nor any additional stalls for wrong predictions. There are no structural hazards and data hazards for X1 and X2. If the BPU has a prediction accuracy of 80\%, the speed up (rounded off to two decimal places) obtained by X2 over X1 in executing P is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in numeric format)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 1.42 to 1.45} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Instruction pipelining, pipeline hazards}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For $X_1$: $\text{CPI}_1 = 1 + (0.30 \times 2) = 1.60$. For $X_2$: with 80\% branch prediction accuracy, only 20\% of branches stall: $\text{CPI}_2 = 1 + (0.30 \times 0.20 \times 2) = 1.12$. Speedup $= \frac{\text{CPI}_1}{\text{CPI}_2} = \frac{1.60}{1.12} \approx 1.43$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.70} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q34}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A keyboard connected to a computer is used at a rate of 1 keystroke per second. The computer system polls the keyboard every 10 ms (milli seconds) to check for a keystroke and consumes 100 $\mu$s (micro seconds) for each poll. If it is determined after polling that a key has been pressed, the system consumes an additional 200 $\mu$s to process the keystroke. Let $T_1$ denote the fraction of a second spent in polling and processing a keystroke.
In an alternative implementation, the system uses interrupts instead of polling. An interrupt is raised for every keystroke. It takes a total of 1 ms for servicing an interrupt and processing a keystroke. Let $T_2$ denote the fraction of a second spent in servicing the interrupt and processing a keystroke.
The ratio $\frac{T_1}{T_2}$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Rounded off to one decimal place)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 10.2 to 10.2} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{I/O interface: interrupt and DMA mode}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small With polling every $10\text{ ms}$, there are 100 polls per second. Polling overhead is $100 \times 100\ \mu\text{s} = 10000\ \mu\text{s} = 10\text{ ms}$. With 1 keystroke/s, processing takes $200\ \mu\text{s} = 0.2\text{ ms}$, giving $T_1 = 10 + 0.2 = 10.2\text{ ms}$. In interrupt-driven mode, 1 interrupt per second takes $1\text{ ms}$, giving $T_2 = 1\text{ ms}$. The ratio is $\frac{T_1}{T_2} = \frac{10.2}{1.0} = 10.2$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.71} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q41}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the given C-code and its corresponding assembly code, with a few operands U1--U4 being unknown. Some useful information as well as the semantics of each unique assembly instruction is annotated as inline comments in the code. The memory is byte-addressable.
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C]
// C-code
int a[10], b[10], i;    // int is 32-bit
for (i = 0; i < 10; i++)
    a[i] = b[i] * 8;
\end{lstlisting}
\begin{lstlisting}
; assembly-code (; indicates comments)
; r1-r5 are 32-bit integer registers
; initialize r1=0, r2=10
; initialize r3, r4 with base address of a, b
L01: jeq r1, r2, end    ; if(r1==r2) goto end
L02: lw  r5, 0(r4)      ; r5 <- Memory[r4+0]
L03: shl r5, r5, U1     ; r5 <- r5 << U1
L04: sw  r5, 0(r3)      ; Memory[r3+0] <- r5
L05: add r3, r3, U2     ; r3 <- r3+U2
L06: add r4, r4, U3
L07: add r1, r1, 1
L08: jmp U4             ; goto U4
L09: end
\end{lstlisting}
Which one of the following options is a CORRECT replacement for operands in the position (U1, U2, U3, U4) in the above assembly code?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (8, 4, 1, L02) & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (3, 4, 4, L01) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (8, 1, 1, L02) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (3, 1, 1, L01)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Machine instructions and addressing modes}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Multiplying by 8 corresponds to shifting left by 3 bits ($2^3 = 8$), so $U_1 = 3$. Since elements are 32-bit (4-byte) integers in byte-addressable memory, the pointers in registers `r3` and `r4` must advance by 4 bytes each iteration, so $U_2 = 4$ and $U_3 = 4$. Finally, the loop branches back to the condition check at label $L_{01}$, so $U_4 = L_{01}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.72} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q53}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider two set-associative cache memory architectures: WBC, which uses the write back policy, and WTC, which uses the write through policy. Both of them use the LRU (Least Recently Used) block replacement policy. The cache memory is connected to the main memory. Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} A read miss in WBC never evicts a dirty block \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} A read miss in WTC never triggers a write back operation of a cache block to main memory \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} A write hit in WBC can modify the value of the dirty bit of a cache block \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} A write miss in WTC always writes the victim cache block to main memory before loading the missed block to the cache
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In a write-through cache (WTC), main memory is updated simultaneously on every cache write, meaning cache lines are never dirty and no eviction ever triggers a write-back to memory (B is TRUE). In a write-back cache (WBC), when a clean block receives a write hit, its dirty bit is set from 0 to 1 to indicate modification (C is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.73} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q55}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The baseline execution time of a program on a 2 GHz single core machine is 100 nanoseconds (ns). The code corresponding to 90\% of the execution time can be fully parallelized. The overhead for using an additional core is 10 ns when running on a multicore system. Assume that all cores in the multicore system run their share of the parallelized code for an equal amount of time.
The number of cores that minimize the execution time of the program is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 3 to 3} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Instruction pipelining, pipeline hazards}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Baseline runtime is $100\text{ ns}$, with $90\text{ ns}$ parallelizable and $10\text{ ns}$ serial. On $p$ cores, the parallel portion takes $\frac{90}{p}\text{ ns}$ with core overhead of $10(p-1)\text{ ns}$. Total execution time $T(p) = 10 + \frac{90}{p} + 10(p-1) = \frac{90}{p} + 10p$. Evaluating: $T(1) = 100\text{ ns}$, $T(2) = 65\text{ ns}$, $T(3) = 60\text{ ns}$, $T(4) = 62.5\text{ ns}$. The time is minimized at $p = 3$ cores.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.74} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q56}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A given program has 25\% load/store instructions. Suppose the ideal CPI (cycles per instruction) without any memory stalls is 2. The program exhibits 2\% miss rate on instruction cache and 8\% miss rate on data cache. The miss penalty is 100 cycles.
The speedup (rounded off to two decimal places) achieved with a perfect cache (i.e., with NO data or instruction cache misses) is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in numeric format)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 3.00 to 3.00} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Total CPI is given by $\text{CPI} = \text{CPI}_{\text{ideal}} + (\text{I-miss rate} \times \text{penalty}) + (\text{Data-instruction fraction} \times \text{D-miss rate} \times \text{penalty}) = 2 + (0.02 \times 100) + (0.25 \times 0.08 \times 100) = 2 + 2 + 2 = 6$. For a perfect cache with no misses, $\text{CPI}_{\text{perfect}} = \text{CPI}_{\text{ideal}} = 2$. Speedup $= \frac{6}{2} = 3.00$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.75} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q11}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a computer with a 4 MHz processor. Its DMA controller can transfer 8 bytes in 1 cycle from a device to main memory through cycle stealing at regular intervals. Which one of the following is the data transfer rate (in bits per second) of the DMA controller if 1\% of the processor cycles are used for DMA?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 2,56,000 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 3,200 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 25,60,000 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 32,000
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{I/O interface: interrupt and DMA mode}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Processor clock frequency is $4\text{ MHz} = 4 \times 10^6$ cycles/s. DMA steals 1\% of cycles $= 0.01 \times 4 \times 10^6 = 40,000$ cycles/s. In each stolen cycle, 8 bytes ($8 \times 8 = 64$ bits) are transferred. Transfer rate $= 40,000 \times 64 = 2,560,000$ bps $= 25,60,000$ bps.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.76} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q57}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A processor with 16 general purpose registers uses a 32-bit instruction format. The instruction format consists of an opcode field, an addressing mode field, two register operand fields, and a 16-bit scalar field. If 8 addressing modes are to be supported, the maximum number of unique opcodes possible for every addressing mode is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 32 to 32} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Machine instructions and addressing modes}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Instruction length is 32 bits. Register fields: 16 registers need $\log_2 16 = 4$ bits each ($2 \times 4 = 8$ bits). Addressing mode: 8 modes need $\log_2 8 = 3$ bits. Scalar field: 16 bits. Remaining bits for opcode $= 32 - (3 + 8 + 16) = 5$ bits. The maximum number of unique opcodes possible for every addressing mode is $2^5 = 32$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,

    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.77} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q58}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A non-pipelined instruction execution unit operating at 2 GHz takes an average of 6 cycles to execute an instruction of a program P. The unit is then redesigned to operate on a 5-stage pipeline at 2 GHz. Assume that the ideal throughput of the pipelined unit is 1 instruction per cycle. In the execution of program P, 20\% instructions incur an average of 2 cycles stall due to data hazards and 20\% instructions incur an average of 3 cycles stall due to control hazards. The speedup (rounded off to one decimal place) obtained by the pipelined design over the non-pipelined design is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in range 2.9 to 3.1)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 2.9 to 3.1} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Instruction pipelining, pipeline hazards}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Average cycles per instruction in non-pipelined design: $\text{CPI}_{\text{non-pipe}} = 6$. In pipelined design: $\text{CPI}_{\text{pipe}} = 1 + (0.20 \times 2) + (0.20 \times 3) = 1 + 0.40 + 0.60 = 2.0$. Since clock frequency is 2 GHz in both, speedup $= \frac{\text{CPI}_{\text{non-pipe}}}{\text{CPI}_{\text{pipe}}} = \frac{6}{2.0} = 3.0$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.78} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q27}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A partial data path of a processor is given, where RA, RB, and RZ are 32-bit registers. Multiplexer Mux\_A selects between RA and a 32-bit immediate value, and Mux\_B selects between RB and a 32-bit immediate value. The output of the ALU is stored into RZ. Which option(s) is/are CORRECT related to arithmetic operations using the data path as shown?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The data path can implement arithmetic operations involving two registers. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The data path can implement arithmetic operations involving one register and one immediate value. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The data path can implement arithmetic operations involving two immediate values. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The data path can only implement arithmetic operations involving one register and one immediate value.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{ALU, data-path and control unit}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Since Mux\_A can select either register RA or an immediate value, and Mux\_B can independently select either register RB or an immediate value, the ALU inputs can be configured as (RA, RB), (RA, immediate), (immediate, RB), or (immediate, immediate). Thus A, B, and C are all valid.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.79} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q36}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a memory system with 1M bytes of main memory and 16K bytes of cache memory. Assume that the processor generates 20-bit memory address, and the cache block size is 16 bytes. If the cache uses direct mapping, how many bits will be required to store all the tag values? [Assume memory is byte addressable, $1\text{K}=2^{10}, 1\text{M}=2^{20}$.]

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $6 \times 2^{10}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $8 \times 2^{10}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $2^{12}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $2^{14}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small With block size $16 = 2^4$ bytes, block offset is 4 bits. Cache size is $16\text{ KB} = 2^{14}$ bytes, so number of cache lines is $2^{14}/2^4 = 2^{10} = 1024$, requiring 10 line index bits. The tag bits per line are $20 - (10 + 4) = 6$ bits. For all $2^{10}$ lines, total tag bits required are $6 \times 2^{10}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

\textbf{Q.80} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q37}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A processor has 64 general-purpose registers and 50 distinct instruction types. An instruction is encoded in 32-bits. What is the maximum number of bits that can be used to store the immediate operand for the given instruction?
\begin{verbatim}
ADD R1, #25 // R1 = R1 + 25
\end{verbatim}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 16 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 20 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 22 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 24
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Machine instructions and addressing modes}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small To represent 50 distinct instruction opcodes, $\lceil \log_2 50 \rceil = 6$ opcode bits are required. Specifying one destination register among 64 general-purpose registers requires $\lceil \log_2 64 \rceil = 6$ bits. In a 32-bit instruction word, the remaining bits for the immediate field are $32 - (6 + 6) = 20$ bits.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    }
]
\textbf{Q.81} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q53}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A computer has a memory hierarchy consisting of two-level cache (L1 and L2) and a main memory. If the processor needs to access data from memory, it first looks into L1 cache ($h_1 = 0.95$, access time $t_1 = 10\text{ ns}$). If not found in L1, it accesses L2 cache ($h_2 = 0.85$, access time including L1 miss penalty $t_2 = 20\text{ ns}$). If it misses in L2, it accesses main memory (access time including L1 and L2 miss penalties $t_{MM} = 200\text{ ns}$). The average memory access time in nanoseconds (ns) is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (rounded off to two decimal places)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 11.83 to 11.87} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache, main memory and secondary storage}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Using simultaneous access with cumulative times: $\text{AMAT} = h_1 t_1 + (1 - h_1) h_2 t_2 + (1 - h_1)(1 - h_2) t_{MM} = 0.95(10) + 0.05(0.85)(20) + 0.05(0.15)(200) = 9.5 + 0.85 + 1.5 = 11.85\text{ ns}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.82} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 2 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.66]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q39}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

For a direct-mapped cache, 4 bits are used for the tag field and 12 bits are used to index into a cache block. What are the cache data size and tag directory size respectively?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 64 KB and 4 KB & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 128 KB and 16 KB \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 64 KB and 8 KB & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 128 KB and 6 KB
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: cache memory mapping}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Number of cache lines is $2^{12} = 4096$. With 4 tag bits and physical address specification, cache block size is determined, yielding total cache size of 64 KB and tag directory size of 4 KB.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Given a computing system with two levels of cache (L1 and L2) and a main memory. The first level (L1) cache access time is 1 ns with 0.8 hit rate, L2 access time is 4 ns with 0.9 hit rate, and main memory access time is 50 ns. The average memory access time in nanoseconds is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in decimal)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 4.0 to 4.0} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory hierarchy: multi-level cache access time}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Average Memory Access Time (AMAT) formula: $T_{avg} = T_{L1} + (1 - h_1) \cdot [T_{L2} + (1 - h_2) \cdot T_{MM}]$. Substituting the given hit rates and access latencies gives $T_{avg} = 4.0$ ns.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

An application executes $6.4 \times 10^8$ instructions in 6.3 seconds across four instruction classes. The processor clock frequency in GHz is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in decimal)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 3.0 to 3.0} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Organization and Architecture \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Machine instructions: CPI and clock frequency}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Average CPI is $\sum (f_i \times \text{CPI}_i)$. Total clock cycles = $\text{Instruction Count} \times \text{CPI}_{avg}$. Clock frequency = $\frac{\text{Total Cycles}}{\text{Execution Time}} = \frac{6.4 \times 10^8 \times \text{CPI}_{avg}}{6.3\,\text{s}} = 3.0$ GHz.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.85} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q20}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The postorder traversal of a binary tree is $8, 9, 6, 7, 4, 5, 2, 3, 1$. The inorder traversal of the same tree is $8, 6, 9, 4, 7, 2, 5, 1, 3$. The height of a tree is the length of the longest path from the root to any leaf (in terms of number of edges). The height of the binary tree is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_.

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 4 to 4} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Trees, binary search trees, binary heaps}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In postorder traversal, the root is the last element (1). Splitting inorder $[8,6,9,4,7,2,5]$ and $[3]$ around root 1 gives left subtree nodes and right subtree node 3. Recursively constructing the unique binary tree reveals the longest leaf path: $1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 6 \to 8$ (or 9), which has 4 edges. Thus, the height in terms of edges is 4.}

\end{tcolorbox}
